## ABSTRACT NO. : 207U: Obstructive Refluxive Megaureter - Dual pathology at Vesico-ureteric Junction is not so uncommon

Category : UROLOGY

Clovey Singhal, Satish K. Aggarwal, Chandrika Kalagotla, Varsha Verma, Kriti Mittal, Shubham

Department of Pediatric Surgery, Sir Ganga Ram Hospital, New Delhi Email ID : clovey.singhal@gmail.com
Megaureter is a common congenital malformation of the ureter. DMSA and MCUG are usually done to pick up scarring and VUR. Reflux is often managed conservatively expecting spontaneous downgrading. DTPA (RDS) is either not done or it is done without a catheter. Often the possibility of concomitant obstruction is not thought of and the kidney may deteriorate over time. We reviewed 7 cases of unilateral VUR where the obstructive pathology at VUJ had been missed.
Materials and Methods: Over 6 months duration, records of patients with ureteric reimplantation were reviewed. Seven cases with dual pathology at the VUJ were further analyzed.
Cases details: 22 ureteric units in 15 cases underwent ureteric reimplantation. Seven all bilateral) (5 boys and 2 girls aged 4 months to 9 years of age) had obstructive and refluxive megaureters. Five had left sided and two had right sided pathology. Evaluation was done with DMSA, DTPA with catheter, USS, MR urography and Cystoscopy with or without RGP. All were referred from elsewhere with recurrent UTIs and deteriorating renal function and progressive megaureters. One had a lower loop ureterostomy performed earlier (He underwent modified Boari flap reimplantation). Five had modified LB Politano reimplanation with tapering. Three had associated PUJ obstruction for which pyeloplasty was also performed - with reverse Sober’s ureterostomy. All had functional improvement. One case had recurrent reflux but no obstruction.
Conclusion: Dual pathology at VUJ is not uncommon. DTPA with catheter and MR Urography can pick up these pathologies and help in surgical planning. Ureteric tapering is likely to be required in most as the ureter is very dilated.

## ABSTRACT NO. : 211U: Management of Concomitant PUJ obstruction and VUJ obstruction

Category : UROLOGY

Shubham, Satish Kumar Aggarwal, Garvita Singh, Chandrika Kalgotla, Varsha, Kriti Mittal, Clovey Singhal

Department of Pediatric Surgery, Sir Ganga Ram Hospital, New Delhi Email ID : shubhamkumar24058@gmail.com
Introduction: Congenital pelviureteric junction obstruction (PUJO) is one of the most common causes of hydronephrosis in children. Its association with vesicoureteric junction anomalies is not very commonly noticed. We present our experience in 8 such cases.
Materials and Methods: Record of 8 patient ( 6 boys 2 girls ) who presented with concomitant PUJO and VUJO , from 2017 to 2023 were reviewed. They underwent Pyeloplasty with reverse sober’s ureterostomy followed by ureteric reimplantation 6 months later.
Cases details: The age range was 2 months to 3 years. Evaluation was with USS, RDS and MR urography and cystoscopy. The 2 month old baby also required ureteroneocystostomy (no formal tunnel creation ) to relieve VUJO soon after pyeloplasty. In the rest Cohen’s or modified LB Politano Reimplantation was done. The ureterostomy stoma was closed later. In two cases the ureterostomy stoma closed spontaneously. Renal function improved in all.
Conclusion: In a case of PUJO, concomitant VUJO should be suspected if there is ureteric dilatation but no reflux. Upper obstruction should be relieved first. Reverse Sober’s ureterostomy achieves ureteric drainage in the interim while waiting for ureteric reimplant. MR Urography gives anatomical details.

## ABSTRACT NO. : 217U: Non operative management of high grade renal trauma - Demography and outcome

Category : UROLOGY

Shreya Rai, J K Mahajan, Shailesh Solanki, Muneer Abas Malik, Nitin James Peters, Akshay Saxena

Department of Pediatric Surgery, Postgraduate Institute of Medical Education and Research, Chandigarh Email ID : shreya123sr96@gmail.com
Aim: Kidneys are involved in 5% of all trauma cases and 24% of abdominal solid organ injuries. The management has transitioned towards more conservative approaches for hemodynamically stable patients. This study analyzes mechanism of injury, management, and outcomes.
Method: This was a retrospective review of conservatively managed renal trauma patients diagnosed between 2003 to 2021. Data were collected from medical records. Imaging grades were classified by radiologists. Variables analyzed included demography, mechanism of injury, grade of renal trauma, related organ injury, management and outcome.
Results: Out of the 15 patients identified, the average age was 6.6years (range 3-10 years) , and most were males (63.3%). Blunt trauma accounted for all cases. Thirty three percent of the patients had hematuria at presentation. The most common renal injuries were grade III (38.4%) while grade IV and V were seen in 30% each. Most patients were treated with non-operative management (NOM) with an average hospital stay of 10 days. Isolated renal trauma was found in 33.3% of cases. One patient developed urinoma which was managed with percutaneous nephrostomy (PCN) followed by double J stent and PCN removal. Another patient had PUJ obstruction which was managed with PCN and pyeloplasty. One patient with grade V renal injury developed pseudoaneurysm with active contrast extravasation needed gel foam embolization. Nephrectomy was performed in 2 cases (13%); one with global infarction of left kidney with hypertension, another had post traumatic PUJO with non-function of kidney. The overall mortality rate (13.3%) was due to hemorrhagic shock and related injuries while rest were discharged with one-third requiring intervention.
Conclusions: Majority of high grade, hemodynamically stable, renal trauma cases can be treated with NOM however, one-third require ancillary interventions to produce better renal functional outcomes. Delayed nephrectomy may be required in few for complications of poorly functioning kidneys.

## ABSTRACT NO. : 241U: Transureteroureterostomy in children: A retrospective study

Category : UROLOGY

H Aneesh Shenoy, Veerabhadra, Karthik S A

Department of Pediatric Surgery, Bangalore Medical College and Research Institute, Bengaluru, Karnataka Email ID : haneeshshenoy@gmail.com
AIMS: To determine theindications, methods, and outcomes of transureteroureterostomy (TUU) in children.
METHODOLOGY: A retrospective study was performed in the Department of Pediatric Surgery at a tertiary centre. The study included children under the age of 18 years who underwent transureteroureterostomy between 2010 and2022.
OBSERVATIONS & RESULTS: A total of 11 children, including 7 boys, were included in the study. The mean age at the time oftransureteroureterostomy was 4.2 ± 2.81 years, with the youngest patient presenting at 5 months and the oldest at 10 years. The most common indication for TUU (55%) was when the lower part of the ureter was used for bladder augmentation. Other indications included congenital short ureter with ectopic insertion, acquired short ureter following excision of paraureteric diverticulum, vascular compromise of the ureter, and when the lower ureter was used as a catheterizable stoma for clean intermittent catheterization. Right to left TUU was performed in 7 children. One child who underwent right to left TUU for posterior urethral valves (PUV) with right grade V vesicoureteral reflux (VUR) and myogenic failure developed bilateral pyonephrosis, which was managed with short-term percutaneous nephrostomy. The remaining children showed positive outcomes and were doing well during follow-up.
Conclusion: Transureteroureterostomy is a reliable and safe procedure that does not compromise the functioning of the other kidney when appropriate patient selection is made. TUU may be considered when bilateral reimplantation is not feasible or when the lower end of the ureter is damaged or being used for bladder augmentation or as a catheterizable stoma.

## ABSTRACT NO. : 243U: Role of Ureterocalycostomy compared to Re-do pyeloplasty in failed secondary PUJO.

Category : UROLOGY

Suvidha Lakshmi M, Vinay Jadhav

Department of Pediatric Surgery, Indira Gandhi Institute Of Child Health, Bengaluru, Karnataka Email ID : suvidhasaggitarius@gmail.com
Aim: To analyse the outcomes of ureterocalycostomy in failed secondary PUJO and secondary long segment ureteric stricture and compare ureterocalycostomy results with re do pyeloplasty in failed secondary PUJO.
Methodology: The Study design is retrospective observational study. Duration of the study is 10 years from January 2013 to December 2022.

All children who underwent ureterocalycostomy for failed secondary PUJO in our institution were included in the study.
All children who underwent Re do pyeloplasties for failed secondary PUJO.

Excluded children are those whose data was not available and lost to follow up.
All children who underwent primary pyeloplasty for PUJO were followed up and failed pyeloplasty diagnosed on Renal scintigraphy were identified and included in the study. Operative details ( ureterocalycostomy or Redo pyeloplasty) were noted. All re do cases had repeat renal scintigraphy and drainage patterns recorded. Non obstructive drainage pattern with or without functional improvement were considered successful.
Results: Total 14 children who underwent ureterocalycostomy for failed secondary PUJO and 21 children who underwent Re do pyeloplasties were included in the study.Age ranged from 4 months to 17years with median age of 5 years . In 70% cases left kidney was affected. Primary procedure was open Anderson hynespyeloplasty in 82% of cases and in 18% laparoscopic pyeloplasty was the primary procedure.All the children whohad ureterocalycostomy had resolution of symptoms, improvement in hydronephrosis on ultrasound andon renal diuretic scintigraphy had non obstructive drainage patterns. Functional improvement were seen in 64% of cases.
In the Re do pyeloplasty group, out of 21 cases, 13 children had a successful outcome with good drainage pattern on renal scintigraphy. Remaining 8 cases showed redo obstruction. In this subset of 8 cases, 4 cases underwent re do re do ureterocalycostomy and 4 underwent nephrectomy for non-functioning kidney with negligible renal parenchyma. These 4 re do re do ureterocalycostomy children on follow up had non obstructive drainage patterns with maintained renal function, hence having a successful outcome.
Conclusion: Ureterocalycostomy is a safe useful and reliable option with good outcomes in children with failed secondary PUJO and in secondary long segment ureteric strictures. Ureterocalycostomy outcomes are better than redo pyeloplasty in failed PUJO cases.

## ABSTRACT NO. : 182U: Transverse Testicular Ectopia : A Rare Case Report

Category : UROLOGY

Vankam Aseesh Varma, Neeraj Tuteja

Department of Pediatric Surgery, Sawai Man Singh Medical College, Jaipur, Rajasthan Email ID : aseeshvarma@gmail.com
Background: Transverse Testicular Ectopia(TTE) is one of the rare urogenital anomalies in which both testes are migrated and descend through a single inguinal canal, one or both testes may be ectopic in the abdomen, the inguinal region or descent to the hemi-scrotum with empty contralateral hemi-scrotum. With fewer than 100 cases reported in English literature, we report this new case found in a 5yr old boy
Case Report: A 5 yr old boy was admitted for bilateral undescended testes. The patient had given complaints of swelling in the right groin. The pregnancy and birth history were unremarkable. Clinical examination showed bilateral undescended testes. Ultrasound examination revealed right and left testis in the Right inguinal region .At surgery, two testes was found in the right inguinal region with two indirect inguinal sacs. Herniotomy was performed for both sacs. The right testis was placed and fixed in the right hemi-scrotum and the left testis was fixed in the left hemi-scrotum through trans-septal window.. The child’s postoperative course was uneventful. A subsequent abdominal ultrasound scan revealed no renal abnormality, and the child has remained well on follow-up.
Conclusion: Transverse Testicular Ectopia is a rare urogenital anomaly with fewer than 100 cases reported in English literature. A good clinical examination in suspicion of TTE with the combination of ultrasound aids the diagnosis. Surgery must be planned keeping in the mind to preserve the fertility. Patients need to be followed up long term in view of fertility issues and probable development of Testicular Cancer.

## ABSTRACT NO. : 189U: Transverse Testicular Ectopia: A case report

Category : UROLOGY

Aboli Raghuvir Hukeri, Aboli Raghuvir Hukeri

Department of Pediatric Surgery, Government Medical College, Aurangabad, Maharashtra Email ID : abolihukeri@gmail.com
Transverse testicula ectopia (TTE) is congenital anamoly with rare incidence (1 in 4 million). It is an entity where both testes are migrated to same inguinal canal and/or hemiscrotum. It has 3 types as per anatomical variations. We present a case of 6 months old baby with 1 testis in left hemiscrotum and another one in left inguinal canal (type 1 TTE). Ultrasound of abdomen and scrotum revealed the same findings without any evidence of mullerian remnants. Child was managed with diagnostic laparoscopy, laparoscopic mobilisation of both testis (without disturbing tesicular and vasal vessels), left orchiopexy and right trans-septal orchiopexy. Review ultrasound of scrotum at 6 months follow up revealed good vascularity of both testes and also had good cosmetic outcome. Now the child is on 6 monthly follow Up. Basic principles for treatment of such case are same as any other orchiopexy and these are fertility preservation, repair of associated anamolies and follow up for malignancy

## ABSTRACT NO. : 215U: Bilateral peri-renal macrocystic lymphatic malformation in pediatric age-unusual presentation

Category : UROLOGY

B. Keerthana, Bijay Kumar Suman, Ramjeevan Singh, Shreyas Dudhani, Amit Kumar Sinha

Department of Pediatric Surgery, All India Institute Of Medical Sciences, Patna, Bihar Email ID : keerthana.bachala@gmail.com
Introduction : Lymphatic malformations are benign lesions, found in children, most common locations are neck and axillary regions.
Methods : We report here a case of bilateral Peri-renal macrocystic lymphatic malformation in a 12-year-old female, masquerading as bilateral renal cysts. A 12-year-old female presented with complaints of pain in right flank for one week associated with fullness in right flank. On evaluation a provisional diagnosis of right perirenal lymphangioma was made and ultrasound guided pigtail drainage done. The pigtail output was 200-300ml daily for a week and it was removed and child was discharged. However, on follow up, CT was done and bilateral peri-renal lymphatic malformation(right>left) was diagnosed. Patient was then planned for exploratory laparotomy which revealed bilateral perirenal cyst, circumscribing both the kidneys completely. Excision of bilateral cysts was done and sent for histopathological evaluation. Cyst Fluid analysis revealed normal creatinine, LDH, total cholesterol, total protein, triglycerides, urea levels. Post-operative period was uneventful. Histopathological examination revealed cystic lesions formed by endothelial cells with focal inflammation, no signs of malignancy were seen. The child was followed up post-operatively and there was no evidence of recurrence. Perirenal lymphatic malformation is a rare entity. The entity is thought to be the result of the obstruction of the perirenal lymphatics.
Conclusion : We are presenting this case for its rarity and unusual presentation. Macro cystic Lymphatic malformations are common diagnosis but around the kidneys, there are only a very few cases reported in literature. The prognosis of the cystic lymphatic malformation is excellent.

## ABSTRACT NO. : 218U: Infantile Hypertrophic Pyloric Stenosis and Y duplication of Urethra with Ano-rectal Malformation

## ABSTRACT NO. : 229U: Traumatic urethrovaginal fistula in children - Abdominoperineal repair

Category : UROLOGY

Josy Thomas, Jai Kumar Mahajan, Shailesh Solanki, Muneer A Malik, Akshay Saxena (Department Of Pediatric Radiology)

Department of Pediatric Surgery, Postgraduate Institute Of Medical Education And Research, Chandigarh Email ID : josy.thomas@hotmail.com
Background: Traumatic urethrovaginal fistulas (UVF) are uncommon injuries in pediatric age group. Treatment of such defects is challenging due to difficult access and paucity of tissue for the secure closure. We used a novel abdomino-perineal approach for managing these patients.
Methods: Two girls, aged 3 and 9 years, presented to our center with urinary incontinence. Both the girls had sustained blunt trauma in road traffic accidents with pelvic fractures and had undergone supra pubic cystostomies (SPC). On examination, urine was seen to be leaking from the vaginal orifices in both these children, however the external openings of both, urethraand vaginawere seen separately. The urethra in either child could be calibrated with an infant feeding tube, which after 3-4 cms was seen to enter the vagina. In both the children urethrocystoscopy showed normal caliber urethra but the urinary bladder could not be entered. With the diagnosis of proximal urethrovaginal fistula, a combined abdomino perineal approach with trans-vesical access was used for exploration. The dissection was started at the uterovesical pouch superiorly and carried along the posterior wall of bladder andanterior wall of uterus and vagina. Simultaneous perineal dissection was performed between the posterior wall of urethra and anterior wall of vagina, to complete separationof bladder and urethra from the vagina. After separation, the fistulous communication was sutured by transvesically for proximal urethra and transvaginal access used for the vaginal defect. A vascularized flap of omentum was inserted between the two suture lines. The per-urethral catheter was removed after 4 weeks. The cystoscopy revealed a well-healed urethra and bladder neck,and both the patients were continent in the follow-up period.
Conclusions: Abdomino-perineal approach provides adequate access for difficult urethra-vaginal fistula repair in young patients and interposition of omental tissue prevents recurrence.

## ABSTRACT NO. : 239U: Vesicoscopic bilateral Cohen’s reimplantation in right renal duplex with VUR

Category : UROLOGY

Ananya Kapoor, Manish Pathak

Department of Pediatric Surgery, All India Institute Of Medical Sciences, Jodhpur, Rajasthan Email ID : ananyakapoor0311@gmail.com
Case details: This video demonstrates Vesicoscopic bilateral Cohen’s reimplantation in the case of a 4-year-old female who is an operated case of vestibular fistula with right duplex system and bilateral VUR with HDUN. DMSA scan showed bilateral cortical scarring.
Procedure: A transcutaneous broad gauge needle was inserted into the bladder under cystoscopic guidance to hitch the bladder dome using 2-0 prolene.
A 5mm port was inserted at the bladder dome and two 3mm ports were placed at the level of the line joining ASIS at midclavicular point under pneumovesicum.
Cystoscopy was used to cannulate 3 Fr ureteral catheters into the duplicated ureters on the right side, and a 6 Fr feeding tube was inserted on the left.
Mucosal incision was given around the orifice to mobilize ureters and subsequently a submucosal tunnel was made across the bladder trigone. Right side common sheath reimplantation along with left cohen’s reimplantation was performed.
The operative duration was 180 minutes.
Postoperative course: Post operative course was uneventful.
At 1 month follow up, USG demonstrated complete resolution of HDUN with preserved kidney function tests.
Conclusion: Vesicoscopic Cohen’s is a safe and feasible minimally invasive procedure even in duplex system.

## ABSTRACT NO. : 240U: Acquired recto-urethral fistula in a case of posterior urethral valve (PUV) - A rare complication and its management

Category : UROLOGY

Mamta Sengar, Parveen K Maan, Vivek Manchanda, Ankur Bharadwaj

Department of Pediatric Surgery, Chacha Nehru Bal Chikitsalaya, Delhi Email ID : mamtasengar@gmail.com
Background: Acquired recto-urethral fistula (RUF) is a rare entity in children, usually occurring due to traumatic, iatrogenic or infective pathology. Iatrogenic recto-urethral fistula following ablation of Posterior Urethral Valve (PUV) is rarely reported in literature.
Case report: One-year old male presented with history of straining at micturition and dribbling of urine since birth. At day 10 of life, patient underwent some cystoscopic procedure at another hospital, following which patient had urinary leak through anal opening, which was first noticed 6 months back by his mother. History of recurrent urinary tract infection (UTI) was present. Ultrasonography of KUB region showed bilateral hydroureteronephrosis and thickened bladder wall. Voiding Cystourethrogram (VCUG) of the child showed dilated posterior urethra with a sudden transition at bulbo-membranous junction, with contrast leakage into rectum suggesting RUF. Cystoscopy revealed residual posterior urethral valves with a small fistulous opening on the right side of verumontanum and sacculated bladder. Supra-pubic cystostomy (SPC) done in same sitting. 2 months later, diverting colostomy and check cystoscopy was performed, which showed a stricture just distal to bulbo-membranous urethral junction. Optical internal urethrotomy was done. 3 months after colostomy, child was taken for RUF repair by anterior sagittal trans-rectal approach (ASTRA), which provided excellent exposure of fistula. One-year follow-up of child showed no recurrence of urinary or fecal incontinence, and post-operative VCUG at 6 months showed no leakage of contrast in rectum.
Conclusion: Acquired RUF is a possible grave complication of PUV ablation in children, Aggressive incision/ ablation of PUV should be avoided in neonates. ASTRA approach provided excellent exposure for fistula repair in our case.
